them sitting in some random place I swear I don’t remember putting them.\n- S009 (Daily_Activities_Forgetfulness): Even with a calendar on the wall and reminders in my phone, I keep spacing out on everyday stuff—like forgetting to pay the electric bill until I get a warning, missing a dentist appointment I booked weeks ago, or realizing at the end of the day that I never called someone back about a repair I promised to schedule.\n▸ Additional Diagnostic Requirements\n- onset_prior_to_age_12_years: Thinking back, even in elementary school my teachers would write on my report cards that I Since then, I've really struggled to concentrate; my mind constantly drifts off when people explain repairs to me, and I have to re-read work orders and safety memos several times just to get the information to stick.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any improvement in your memory or concentration when you're in a less stressful environment or when the tasks are less complex, compared to your current work situation?\n\nPatient: To be honest, I'm not really sure because I haven't had much of a break from stress lately to compare. These struggles feel pretty constant and are really hard to shake off, even when I am trying my absolute best. It just feels like it is always happening, no matter what I'm doing.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your difficulty with remembering and concentrating is more related to the stress and overwhelm of your new responsibilities, or do you feel like there's something fundamentally different about how your brain is processing information compared to before?\n\nPatient: The stress of the divorce and my new responsibilities over the last 11 months has definitely made things much worse. But looking back, I realize this isn't entirely new—even in elementary school, my teachers wrote that I was always spacing out, losing worksheets, and forgetting my homework. I think my brain has always worked this way, but with all the new demands, I just can't manage to compensate for it anymore.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me more about what happens when you 'space out' - are you completely unaware of your surroundings, or are you just having trouble focusing on the task at hand?"

It just feels like it is always happening, no matter what I'm doing.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your difficulty with remembering and concentrating is more related to the stress and overwhelm of your new responsibilities, or do you feel like there's something fundamentally different about how your brain is processing information compared to before?\n\nPatient: The stress of the divorce and my new responsibilities over the last 11 months has definitely made things much worse. But looking back, I realize this isn't entirely new—even in elementary school, my teachers wrote that I was always spacing out, losing worksheets, and forgetting my homework. I think my brain has always worked this way, but with all the new demands, I just can't manage to compensate for it anymore.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me more about what happens when you 'space out' - are you completely unaware of your surroundings, or are you just having trouble focusing on the task at hand?\n\nPatient: I'm completely awake and aware of my surroundings, but my mind just drifts away from what's right in front of me. For instance, I'll look directly at someone at work and nod, but their words don't actually register in my head. Other times, I'll read all the way to the bottom of a work order only to realize I didn't absorb a single thing and have to start over.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever experienced periods of excessive restlessness, fidgeting, or an inability to stay seated, especially in quiet environments?"
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
No, I've never really struggled with restlessness, fidgeting, or staying seated. My issues are much more about my focus, attention, and memory rather than any kind of physical hyperactivity.

For instance, I'll look directly at someone at work and nod, but their words don't actually register in my head. Other times, I'll read all the way to the bottom of a work order only to realize I didn't absorb a single thing and have to start over.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever experienced periods of excessive restlessness, fidgeting, or an inability to stay seated, especially in quiet environments?\n\nPatient: No, I've never really struggled with restlessness, fidgeting, or staying seated. My issues are much more about my focus, attention, and memory rather than any kind of physical hyperactivity.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever found yourself interrupting others, blurting out answers, or having difficulty waiting for your turn in conversations?\n\nPatient: No, I don't really struggle with interrupting others or blurting things out in conversations. My issues are much more about spacing out, losing track of my things, and forgetting what I'm supposed to be doing."

My overall mood is okay, but managing all of this on my own is just wearing me down.\n\nDoctor: Can you describe any specific situations or events that triggered these memory problems, and have you noticed any difficulty with concentration or learning new information?\n\nPatient: Things really got worse about a year ago after my divorce, when I moved and took on a lead maintenance role with much more complex tasks. Since then, I've really struggled to concentrate; my mind constantly drifts off when people explain repairs to me, and I have to re-read work orders and safety memos several times just to get the information to stick.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any improvement in your memory or concentration when you're in a less stressful environment or when the tasks are less complex, compared to your current work situation?\n\nPatient: To be honest, I'm not really sure because I haven't had much of a break from stress lately to compare. But even with simple, everyday tasks at home like paying bills or finding my keys, I still find myself struggling and spacing out just as much.\n\nDoctor: Can you think of any times or situations where your memory or concentration seemed to work normally, even if just for a short while, since you started experiencing these problems?\n\nPatient: I can't really think of any times where my memory or concentration has felt completely normal lately. These struggles feel pretty constant and are really hard to shake off, even when I am trying my absolute best. It just feels like it is always happening, no matter what I'm doing.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your difficulty with remembering and concentrating is more related to the stress and overwhelm of your new responsibilities, or do you feel like there's something fundamentally different about how your brain is processing information compared to before?\n\nPatient: The stress of the divorce and my new responsibilities over the last 11 months has definitely made things much worse. But looking back, I realize this isn't entirely new—even in elementary school, my teachers wrote that I was always spacing out, losing worksheets, and forgetting my homework. I think my brain has always worked this way, but with all the new demands, I just can't manage to compensate for it anymore.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me more about what happens when you 'space out' - are you completely unaware of your surroundings, or are you just having trouble focusing on the task at hand?\n\nPatient: I'm completely awake and aware of my surroundings, but my mind just drifts away from what's right in front of me. For instance, I'll look directly at someone at work and nod, but their words don't actually register in my head. Other times, I'll read all the way to the bottom of a work order only to realize I didn't absorb a single thing and have to start over.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever experienced periods of excessive restlessness, fidgeting, or an inability to stay seated, especially in quiet environments?\n\nPatient: No, I've never really struggled with restlessness, fidgeting, or staying seated. My issues are much more about my focus, attention, and memory rather than any kind of physical hyperactivity.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever found yourself interrupting others, blurting out answers, or having difficulty waiting for your turn in conversations?\n\nPatient: No, I don't really struggle with interrupting others or blurting things out in conversations. My issues are much more about spacing out, losing track of my things, and forgetting what I'm supposed to be doing.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me if you've ever been diagnosed with or treated for attention deficit hyperactivity disorder, or ADHD, in the past, perhaps during your childhood or teenage years?\n\nPatient: No, I've never been officially diagnosed or treated for ADHD before, and this is my first time seeking an evaluation. But looking back, my elementary school teachers always wrote on my report cards that I was smart but constantly spaced out, lost my worksheets, and forgot to hand in homework I'd already finished."

Since then, I've really struggled to concentrate; my mind constantly drifts off when people explain repairs to me, and I have to re-read work orders and safety memos several times just to get the information to stick.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any improvement in your memory or concentration when you're in a less stressful environment or when the tasks are less complex, compared to your current work situation?\n\nPatient: To be honest, I'm not really sure because I haven't had much of a break from stress lately to compare. But even with simple, everyday tasks at home like paying bills or finding my keys, I still find myself struggling and spacing out just as much.\n\nDoctor: Can you think of any times or situations where your memory or concentration seemed to work normally, even if just for a short while, since you started experiencing these problems?\n\nPatient: I can't really think of any times where my memory or concentration has felt completely normal lately. These struggles feel pretty constant and are really hard to shake off, even when I am trying my absolute best. It just feels like it is always happening, no matter what I'm doing.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your difficulty with remembering and concentrating is more related to the stress and overwhelm of your new responsibilities, or do you feel like there's something fundamentally different about how your brain is processing information compared to before?\n\nPatient: The stress of the divorce and my new responsibilities over the last 11 months has definitely made things much worse. But looking back, I realize this isn't entirely new—even in elementary school, my teachers wrote that I was always spacing out, losing worksheets, and forgetting my homework. I think my brain has always worked this way, but with all the new demands, I just can't manage to compensate for it anymore.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me more about what happens when you 'space out' - are you completely unaware of your surroundings, or are you just having trouble focusing on the task at hand?\n\nPatient: I'm completely awake and aware of my surroundings, but my mind just drifts away from what's right in front of me. For instance, I'll look directly at someone at work and nod, but their words don't actually register in my head. Other times, I'll read all the way to the bottom of a work order only to realize I didn't absorb a single thing and have to start over.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever experienced periods of excessive restlessness, fidgeting, or an inability to stay seated, especially in quiet environments?\n\nPatient: No, I've never really struggled with restlessness, fidgeting, or staying seated. My issues are much more about my focus, attention, and memory rather than any kind of physical hyperactivity.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever found yourself interrupting others, blurting out answers, or having difficulty waiting for your turn in conversations?\n\nPatient: No, I don't really struggle with interrupting others or blurting things out in conversations. My issues are much more about spacing out, losing track of my things, and forgetting what I'm supposed to be doing.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me if you've ever been diagnosed with or treated for attention deficit hyperactivity disorder, or ADHD, in the past, perhaps during your childhood or teenage years?\n\nPatient: No, I've never been officially diagnosed or treated for ADHD before, and this is my first time seeking an evaluation. But looking back, my elementary school teachers always wrote on my report cards that I was smart but constantly spaced out, lost my worksheets, and forgot to hand in homework I'd already finished.\n\nDoctor: You mentioned that your difficulties with focus and memory have been present since elementary school, but they've worsened over the past year with increased stress and responsibilities. Can you describe how your symptoms have changed or progressed over time, especially in relation to significant life events or stressors?\n\nPatient: In childhood, my spacing out and losing homework were mostly confined to school, and I managed to get by. But after my divorce a year ago, moving into my own place and taking on a lead maintenance role created a level of logistical pressure I'd never faced before. Managing all of my own household, finances, and a demanding job alone made my forgetfulness and half-finished tasks much worse and harder to handle over the last 11 months."

Subtypes: Cleaning or Washing Compulsions, Checking Compulsions, Ordering and Arranging Compulsions, Mental Acts, Repeating Rituals\n[S067] Preoccupation_with_Appearance: Preoccupation with one or more perceived defects or flaws in physical appearance that are not observable or appear slight to others. Subtypes: Intrusive Memories, Distressing Dreams, Dissociative Reactions (Flashbacks), Psychological Distress at Cues, Physiological Reactions to Cues\n[S070] Trauma_Avoidance: Persistent avoidance of stimuli associated with the traumatic event.  Subtypes: Internal Avoidance, External Avoidance\n[S071] Trauma_Related_Amnesia: Inability to remember an important aspect of the traumatic event (typically due to dissociative amnesia and not to other factors like head injury or alcohol).  Subtypes: Fragmented Memory, Generalized Amnesia\n[S072] Negative_Beliefs: Persistent and exaggerated negative beliefs or expectations about oneself, others, or the world.  Subtypes: Negative Self-Beliefs, Negative Beliefs about Others, Negative Worldview\n[S073] Distorted_Cognitions_With_Blame: Persistent, distorted cognitions about the cause or consequences of the traumatic event that lead the individual to blame himself/herself or others.  Subtypes: Self-Blame, Blaming Others\n[S074] Persistent_Negative_Emotional_State: Persistent negative emotional state (e.g., fear, horror, anger, guilt, or shame).  Subtypes: Persistent Fear, Persistent Horror, Persistent Anger, Persistent Guilt, Persistent Shame\n[S075] Social_Detachment: Feelings of detachment or estrangement from others.  Subtypes: Emotional Detachment, Social Isolation, Difficulty with Intimacy, Loss of Belonging\n[S076] Inability_Positive_Emotions: Persistent inability to experience positive emotions (e.g., inability to experience happiness, satisfaction, or loving feelings).  Subtypes: Anhedonia for Happiness, Lack of Satisfaction, Inability to Feel Love, Emotional Numbness\n[S077] Irritability_Aggression: Irritable behavior and angry outbursts (with little or no provocation) typically expressed as verbal or physical aggression.  Subtypes: Impulsive Anger, Verbal Aggression, Physical Aggression, Chronic Irritability\n[S078] Reckless_Self_Destructive_Behavior: Reckless or self-destructive behavior pattern that endangers one's safety or health.  Subtypes: Risky Behaviors, Substance Abuse, Indisicriminate Sexual Behavior, Self-Harm\n[S079] Hypervigilance: Persistent state of being alert and tense about the surrounding environment.
